<p><b>Nicaragua</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˌ/: secondary stress follows">ˌ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/ɑː/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;father&#39;">ɑː</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="&#39;w&#39; in &#39;wind&#39;">w</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span>-<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span></span>-,<span class="wrap"> </span>-<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/j/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;yes&#39;">j</span><span title="/u/: &#39;u&#39; in &#39;influence&#39;">u</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Nicaragua.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" title="En-us-Nicaragua.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span>; <small>Spanish:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="es-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Spanish" title="Help:IPA/Spanish">[nikaˈɾaɣwa]</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:ES-pe_-_Nicaragua.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dc/ES-pe_-_Nicaragua.ogg" class="internal" title="ES-pe - Nicaragua.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span>), officially the <b>Republic of Nicaragua</b> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:ES-pe_-_Rep%C3%BAblica_de_Nicaragua.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8c/ES-pe_-_Rep%C3%BAblica_de_Nicaragua.ogg" class="internal" title="ES-pe - República de Nicaragua.ogg"><i class="unicode" lang="es">República de Nicaragua</i></a></span>&#160;<small class="metadata audiolinkinfo" style="cursor:help;">(<a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Media_help" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:Media help"><span style="cursor:help;">help</span></a>·<a href="/wiki/File:ES-pe_-_Rep%C3%BAblica_de_Nicaragua.ogg" title="File:ES-pe - República de Nicaragua.ogg"><span style="cursor:help;">info</span></a>)</small></span>), is the largest <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">country</a> in <a href="/wiki/Central_America" title="Central America">Central America</a>, bordered by <a href="/wiki/Honduras" title="Honduras">Honduras</a> to the north, the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean_Sea" title="Caribbean Sea">Caribbean</a> to the east, <a href="/wiki/Costa_Rica" title="Costa Rica">Costa Rica</a> to the south, and the Pacific Ocean to the west. <a href="/wiki/Managua" title="Managua">Managua</a> is the country's capital and largest city. As of 2015<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, it was estimated to be the <a href="/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_in_Central_America#Largest_cities_proper" title="List of largest cities in Central America">second largest city in Central America</a>. Nicaragua's multiethnic population of six million includes people of mestizo, indigenous, European and African heritage. The main language is Spanish. Indigenous tribes on the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> speak their own languages and English.
</p><p>Originally inhabited by various indigenous cultures since ancient times, the region was conquered by the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a> in the 16th century. Nicaragua gained independence from Spain in 1821. The Mosquito Coast followed a different historical path, being colonized by the English in the 17th century and later coming under British rule. It became an autonomous territory of Nicaragua in 1860 and its northernmost part was transferred to <a href="/wiki/Honduras" title="Honduras">Honduras</a> in 1960. Since its independence, Nicaragua has undergone periods of political unrest, dictatorship, occupation and fiscal crisis, including the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution" title="Nicaraguan Revolution">Nicaraguan Revolution</a> of the 1960s and 1970s and the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution#Contra_War" title="Nicaraguan Revolution">Contra War</a> of the 1980s.
</p><p>The mixture of cultural traditions has generated substantial diversity in folklore, cuisine, music, and literature, particularly the latter, given the literary contributions of Nicaraguan poets and writers such as <a href="/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo" title="Rubén Darío">Rubén Darío</a>. Known as the "land of lakes and volcanoes",<sup id="cite_ref-Brierley_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brierley-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wallace_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wallace-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Nicaragua is also home to the <a href="/wiki/Bosaw%C3%A1s_Biosphere_Reserve" title="Bosawás Biosphere Reserve">Bosawás Biosphere Reserve</a>, the second-largest rainforest of the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The biological diversity, warm tropical climate and active volcanoes make Nicaragua an increasingly popular <a href="/wiki/Tourist_destination" class="mw-redirect" title="Tourist destination">tourist destination</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Nicaragua co-founded the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> and is also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">Organization of American States</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Peoples_of_Our_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolivarian Alliance for the Peoples of Our America">Bolivarian Alliance for the Peoples of Our America</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Latin_American_and_Caribbean_States" title="Community of Latin American and Caribbean States">Community of Latin American and Caribbean States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>There are two prevailing theories on how the name "Nicaragua" came to be. The first is that the name was coined by Spanish colonists based on the name <a href="/wiki/Nicarao_(cacique)" title="Nicarao (cacique)">Nicarao</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Ideal_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ideal-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> who was the chieftain or <a href="/wiki/Cacique" title="Cacique">cacique</a> of a powerful indigenous tribe encountered by the Spanish <a href="/wiki/Conquistador" title="Conquistador">conquistador</a> <a href="/wiki/Gil_Gonz%C3%A1lez_D%C3%A1vila" title="Gil González Dávila">Gil González Dávila</a> during his entry into southwestern Nicaragua in 1522. This theory holds that the name Nicaragua was formed from Nicarao and <i>agua</i> (Spanish for "water"), to reference the fact that there are two large lakes and several other bodies of water within the country.<sup id="cite_ref-Pueblo_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pueblo-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> However, as of 2002, it was determined that the cacique's real name was Macuilmiquiztli, which meant "Five Deaths" in the <a href="/wiki/Nahuatl" title="Nahuatl">Nahuatl</a> language, rather than Nicarao.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Sanchez_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sanchez-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Encuentro_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encuentro-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The second theory is that the country's name comes from any of the following Nahuatl words: <i>nic-anahuac</i>, which meant "<a href="/wiki/Anahuac_(Aztec)" title="Anahuac (Aztec)">Anahuac</a> reached this far", or "the <a href="/wiki/Nahuas" title="Nahuas">Nahuas</a> came this far", or "those who come from Anahuac came this far"; <i>nican-nahua</i>, which meant "here are the Nahuas"; or <i>nic-atl-nahuac</i>, which meant "here by the water" or "surrounded by water".<sup id="cite_ref-Ideal_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ideal-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Pueblo_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pueblo-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Choque_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Choque-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Nicaragua" title="History of Nicaragua">History of Nicaragua</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pre-Columbian_history">Pre-Columbian history</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Pre-Columbian history">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg/170px-Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg/255px-Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg/340px-Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2736" data-file-height="3648" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_Ometepe_p%C3%A9troglyphes_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An ancient <a href="/wiki/Petroglyph" title="Petroglyph">petroglyph</a> on <a href="/wiki/Ometepe_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Ometepe Island">Ometepe Island</a></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Paleo-Americans" class="mw-redirect" title="Paleo-Americans">Paleo-Americans</a> first inhabited what is now known as Nicaragua as far back as 12,000 BCE.<sup id="cite_ref-Dall2005_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dall2005-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In later <a href="/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era" title="Pre-Columbian era">pre-Columbian</a> times, Nicaragua's <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">indigenous people</a> were part of the <a href="/wiki/Intermediate_Area" title="Intermediate Area">Intermediate Area</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 33">&#58;&#8202;33&#8202;</span></sup> between the <a href="/wiki/Mesoamerica" title="Mesoamerica">Mesoamerican</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andes" title="Andes">Andean</a> cultural regions, and within the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Isthmo-Colombian_Area" title="Isthmo-Colombian Area">Isthmo-Colombian Area</a>. Nicaragua's central region and its Caribbean coast were inhabited by <a href="/wiki/Macro-Chibchan_languages" title="Macro-Chibchan languages">Macro-Chibchan language</a> ethnic groups such as the <a href="/wiki/Miskito_people" title="Miskito people">Miskito</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rama_people" title="Rama people">Rama</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mayagna" class="mw-redirect" title="Mayagna">Mayangna</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cacaopera_people" title="Cacaopera people">Matagalpas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 20">&#58;&#8202;20&#8202;</span></sup> They had coalesced in Central America and migrated both to and from present-day northern Colombia and nearby areas.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Their food came primarily from hunting and gathering, but also fishing and <a href="/wiki/Slash-and-burn" title="Slash-and-burn">slash-and-burn</a> agriculture.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 33">&#58;&#8202;33&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 65">&#58;&#8202;65&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>At the end of the 15th century, western Nicaragua was inhabited by several indigenous peoples related by culture to the Mesoamerican civilizations of the <a href="/wiki/Aztecs" title="Aztecs">Aztec</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maya_peoples" title="Maya peoples">Maya</a>, and by language to the <a href="/wiki/Mesoamerican_language_area" title="Mesoamerican language area">Mesoamerican language area</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC1_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC1-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The Chorotegas were <a href="/wiki/Mangue_language" title="Mangue language">Mangue language</a> ethnic groups who had arrived in Nicaragua from what is now the Mexican state of <a href="/wiki/Chiapas" title="Chiapas">Chiapas</a> sometime around 800 CE.<sup id="cite_ref-Choque_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Choque-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 26–33">&#58;&#8202;26–33&#8202;</span></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Nicarao_people" title="Nicarao people">Nicarao people</a> were a branch of <a href="/wiki/Nahuas" title="Nahuas">Nahuas</a> who spoke the <a href="/wiki/Nawat_language" title="Nawat language">Nawat</a> dialect and also came from Chiapas, around 1200 CE.<sup id="cite_ref-Campbell1985_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Campbell1985-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Prior to that, the Nicaraos had been associated with the <a href="/wiki/Toltec" title="Toltec">Toltec</a> civilization.<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 26–33">&#58;&#8202;26–33&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Campbell1985_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Campbell1985-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Both Chorotegas and Nicaraos originated in Mexico's <a href="/wiki/Cholula_(Mesoamerican_site)" title="Cholula (Mesoamerican site)">Cholula</a> valley,<sup id="cite_ref-Campbell1985_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Campbell1985-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> and migrated south.<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 26–33">&#58;&#8202;26–33&#8202;</span></sup> A third group, the <a href="/wiki/Subtiaba_language" title="Subtiaba language">Subtiabas</a>, were an <a href="/wiki/Oto-Manguean_languages" title="Oto-Manguean languages">Oto-Manguean</a> people who migrated from the Mexican state of <a href="/wiki/Guerrero" title="Guerrero">Guerrero</a> around 1200 CE.<sup id="cite_ref-Campbell1997_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Campbell1997-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 159">&#58;&#8202;159&#8202;</span></sup> Additionally, there were trade-related colonies in Nicaragua set up by the Aztecs starting in the 14th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 26–33">&#58;&#8202;26–33&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Spanish_era_.281523.E2.80.931821.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Spanish_era_(1523–1821)">Spanish era (1523–1821)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Spanish era (1523–1821)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas" title="Spanish colonization of the Americas">Spanish colonization of the Americas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Spanish_conquest_of_Nicaragua" title="Spanish conquest of Nicaragua">Spanish conquest of Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG/220px-06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG/330px-06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG/440px-06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3210" data-file-height="2328" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:06.Plaza_de_la_Independencia_de_Granada.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Colonial <a href="/wiki/Granada,_Nicaragua" title="Granada, Nicaragua">City of Granada</a> near <a href="/wiki/Lake_Nicaragua" title="Lake Nicaragua">Lake Nicaragua</a> is one of the most visited sites in Central America.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1502, on his fourth voyage, <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a> became the first European known to have reached what is now Nicaragua as he sailed southeast toward the <a href="/wiki/Isthmus_of_Panama" title="Isthmus of Panama">Isthmus of Panama</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 193">&#58;&#8202;193&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 92">&#58;&#8202;92&#8202;</span></sup> Columbus explored the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> on the Atlantic side of Nicaragua<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> but did not encounter any indigenous people. 20 years later, the Spaniards returned to Nicaragua, this time to its southwestern part. The first attempt to conquer Nicaragua was by the conquistador <a href="/wiki/Gil_Gonz%C3%A1lez_D%C3%A1vila" title="Gil González Dávila">Gil González Dávila</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-EBH_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBH-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> who had arrived in Panama in January 1520. In 1522, González Dávila ventured to the area that later became the <a href="/wiki/Rivas_Department" title="Rivas Department">Rivas Department</a> of Nicaragua.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 35">&#58;&#8202;35&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 92">&#58;&#8202;92&#8202;</span></sup> There he encountered an indigenous Nahua tribe led by chief Macuilmiquiztli, whose name has sometimes been erroneously referred to as "<a href="/wiki/Nicarao_(cacique)" title="Nicarao (cacique)">Nicarao</a>" or "Nicaragua". The tribe's capital was Quauhcapolca.<sup id="cite_ref-Encuentro_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encuentro-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-HealyPohl1980_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HealyPohl1980-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Dyck2015_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dyck2015-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> González Dávila conversed with Macuilmiquiztli thanks to two indigenous interpreters who had learned Spanish, whom he had brought along.<sup id="cite_ref-Sanchez_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sanchez-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> After exploring and gathering gold<sup id="cite_ref-Encuentro_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encuentro-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 35">&#58;&#8202;35&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 55">&#58;&#8202;55&#8202;</span></sup> in the fertile western valleys, González Dávila and his men were attacked and driven off by the Chorotega, led by chief <a href="/wiki/Diriangen" class="mw-redirect" title="Diriangen">Diriangén</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Encuentro_26-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encuentro-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The Spanish tried to convert the tribes to Christianity; Macuilmiquiztli's tribe was baptized,<sup id="cite_ref-Encuentro_26-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encuentro-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 86">&#58;&#8202;86&#8202;</span></sup> but Diriangén was openly hostile to the Spaniards. Western Nicaragua, at the Pacific Coast, became a port and shipbuilding facility for the Galleons plying the waters between Manila, Philippines and Acapulco, Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first Spanish permanent settlements were founded in 1524.<sup id="cite_ref-EBH_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBH-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> That year, the conquistador <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Hern%C3%A1ndez_de_C%C3%B3rdoba_(founder_of_Nicaragua)" title="Francisco Hernández de Córdoba (founder of Nicaragua)">Francisco Hernández de Córdoba</a> founded two of Nicaragua's main cities: <a href="/wiki/Granada,_Nicaragua" title="Granada, Nicaragua">Granada</a> on <a href="/wiki/Lake_Nicaragua" title="Lake Nicaragua">Lake Nicaragua</a>, and then <a href="/wiki/Le%C3%B3n,_Nicaragua" title="León, Nicaragua">León</a>, west of <a href="/wiki/Lake_Managua" title="Lake Managua">Lake Managua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 35, 193">&#58;&#8202;35,&#8202;193&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 92">&#58;&#8202;92&#8202;</span></sup> Córdoba soon built defenses for the cities and fought against incursions by other conquistadors.<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 92">&#58;&#8202;92&#8202;</span></sup> Córdoba was later publicly <a href="/wiki/Decapitation" title="Decapitation">beheaded</a> for having defied his superior, <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Arias_D%C3%A1vila" title="Pedro Arias Dávila">Pedro Arias Dávila</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 35">&#58;&#8202;35&#8202;</span></sup> Córdoba's tomb and remains were discovered in 2000 in the <a href="/wiki/Le%C3%B3n_Viejo" title="León Viejo">ruins of León Viejo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ET_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ET-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The clashes among Spanish forces did not impede their destruction of the indigenous people and their culture. The series of battles came to be known as the "War of the Captains".<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Pedro Arias Dávila was a winner;<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 35">&#58;&#8202;35&#8202;</span></sup> although he lost control of Panama, he moved to Nicaragua and established his base in León.<sup id="cite_ref-Whisnant2000_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Whisnant2000-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In 1527, León became the capital of the colony.<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 93">&#58;&#8202;93&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Whisnant2000_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Whisnant2000-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Through diplomacy, Arias Dávila became the colony's first governor.<sup id="cite_ref-ET_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ET-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Without women in their parties,<sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 123">&#58;&#8202;123&#8202;</span></sup> the Spanish conquerors took Nahua and Chorotega wives and partners, beginning the multiethnic mix of indigenous and European stock now known as "<i><a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">mestizo</a></i>", which constitutes the great majority of the population in western Nicaragua.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC1_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC1-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Many indigenous people were killed by European <a href="/wiki/Infectious_disease" class="mw-redirect" title="Infectious disease">infectious diseases</a>, compounded by neglect by the Spaniards, who controlled their subsistence.<sup id="cite_ref-EBH_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBH-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Many other indigenous peoples were captured and transported as slaves to Panama and Peru between 1526 and 1540.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 193">&#58;&#8202;193&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Newson_33-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newson-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 104–105">&#58;&#8202;104–105&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In 1610, the <a href="/wiki/Momotombo" title="Momotombo">Momotombo</a> volcano erupted, destroying the city of León.<sup id="cite_ref-Geomorph_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geomorph-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The city was rebuilt northwest of the original,<sup id="cite_ref-Whisnant2000_48-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Whisnant2000-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Geomorph_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geomorph-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> which is now known as the <a href="/wiki/Le%C3%B3n_Viejo" title="León Viejo">ruins of León Viejo</a>. During the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>, Central America was subject to conflict between Britain and Spain. British navy admiral <a href="/wiki/Horatio_Nelson" class="mw-redirect" title="Horatio Nelson">Horatio Nelson</a> led expeditions in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_San_Fernando_de_Omoa" title="Battle of San Fernando de Omoa">Battle of San Fernando de Omoa</a> in 1779 and on the <a href="/wiki/San_Juan_Expedition_(1780)" title="San Juan Expedition (1780)">San Juan River in 1780</a>, the latter of which had temporary success before being abandoned due to disease.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Independent_Nicaragua_from_1821_to_1909">Independent Nicaragua from 1821 to 1909</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Independent Nicaragua from 1821 to 1909">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:392px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png/390px-Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png" decoding="async" width="390" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png/585px-Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png/780px-Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="750" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Political_Evolution_of_Central_America_and_the_Caribbean_1830_na.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Central_America" title="Federal Republic of Central America">Federal Republic of Central America</a> and British colony of the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> in 1830</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg/220px-La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg/330px-La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg/440px-La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2714" data-file-height="1552" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:La_Pedrada_de_Andres_Castro.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Painting of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_San_Jacinto_(1856)" title="Battle of San Jacinto (1856)">Battle of San Jacinto</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Filibuster_War" title="Filibuster War">Filibuster War</a></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Independence_of_Central_America" title="Act of Independence of Central America">Act of Independence of Central America</a> dissolved the <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Guatemala" title="Captaincy General of Guatemala">Captaincy General of Guatemala</a> in September 1821, and Nicaragua soon <a href="/wiki/Central_America_under_Mexican_rule" title="Central America under Mexican rule">became part</a> of the <a href="/wiki/First_Mexican_Empire" title="First Mexican Empire">First Mexican Empire</a>. In July 1823, after the overthrow of the Mexican monarchy in March of the same year, Nicaragua joined the newly formed <a href="/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Central_America" title="Federal Republic of Central America">United Provinces of Central America</a>, country later known as the Federal Republic of Central America. Nicaragua definitively became an independent republic in 1838.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The early years of independence were characterized by rivalry between the <a href="/wiki/Constitutionalist_Liberal_Party" title="Constitutionalist Liberal Party">Liberal</a> elite of León and the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(Nicaragua)" title="Conservative Party (Nicaragua)">Conservative</a> elite of Granada, which often degenerated into civil war, particularly during the 1840s and 1850s. <a href="/wiki/Managua" title="Managua">Managua</a> rose to undisputed preeminence as the nation's capital in 1852 to allay the rivalry between the two feuding cities.<sup id="cite_ref-Capital_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Capital-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Following the start (1848) of the <a href="/wiki/California_Gold_Rush" title="California Gold Rush">California Gold Rush</a>, Nicaragua provided a route for travelers from the eastern United States to journey to <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a> by sea, via the <a href="/wiki/San_Juan_River_(Nicaragua)" title="San Juan River (Nicaragua)">San Juan River</a> and Lake Nicaragua.<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 81">&#58;&#8202;81&#8202;</span></sup> Invited by the Liberals in 1855 to join their struggle against the Conservatives, the American adventurer and <a href="/wiki/Filibuster_(military)" title="Filibuster (military)">filibuster</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Walker_(filibuster)" title="William Walker (filibuster)">William Walker</a> set himself up as <a href="/wiki/President_of_Nicaragua" title="President of Nicaragua">President of Nicaragua</a> after conducting a farcical election in 1856; his presidency lasted less than a year.<sup id="cite_ref-mined_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mined-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Military forces from Costa Rica, Honduras, El Salvador, Guatemala, and Nicaragua itself united to drive Walker out of Nicaragua in 1857,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> bringing three decades of Conservative rule.
</p><p>Great Britain, which had claimed the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Protectorate" title="Protectorate">protectorate</a> since 1655, delegated the area to Honduras in 1859 before transferring it to Nicaragua in 1860. The Mosquito Coast remained an <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_area" class="mw-redirect" title="Autonomous area">autonomous area</a> until 1894. <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Santos_Zelaya" title="José Santos Zelaya">José Santos Zelaya</a>, President of Nicaragua from 1893 to 1909, negotiated the integration of the Mosquito Coast into Nicaragua. In his honor, the region became "<a href="/wiki/Zelaya_Department" title="Zelaya Department">Zelaya Department</a>".
</p><p>Throughout the late 19th-century, the United States and several European powers considered various schemes to link the Pacific Ocean to the Atlantic by building a <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua_Canal" title="Nicaragua Canal">canal across Nicaragua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="United_States_occupation_.281909.E2.80.931933.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="United_States_occupation_(1909–1933)">United States occupation (1909–1933)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: United States occupation (1909–1933)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Nicaragua" title="United States occupation of Nicaragua">United States occupation of Nicaragua</a></div>
<p>In 1909, the United States supported the conservative-led forces rebelling against President Zelaya. U.S. motives included differences over the proposed <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua_Canal" title="Nicaragua Canal">Nicaragua Canal</a>, Nicaragua's potential to destabilize the region, and Zelaya's attempts to regulate foreign access to Nicaraguan natural resources. On November 18, 1909, U.S. warships were sent to the area after 500 revolutionaries (including two Americans) were executed by order of Zelaya. The U.S. justified the intervention by claiming to protect U.S. lives and property. Zelaya resigned later that year.
</p><p>In August 1912, the President of Nicaragua, <a href="/wiki/Adolfo_D%C3%ADaz" title="Adolfo Díaz">Adolfo Díaz</a>, requested the secretary of war, General <a href="/wiki/Luis_Mena_(Nicaraguan_politician)" title="Luis Mena (Nicaraguan politician)">Luis Mena</a>, to resign for fear he was leading an insurrection. Mena fled Managua with his brother, the chief of police of Managua, to start an insurrection. After Mena's troops captured steam boats of an American company, the U.S. delegation asked President Díaz to ensure the safety of American citizens and property during the insurrection. He replied he could not, and asked the U.S. to intervene in the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps" title="United States Marine Corps">U.S. Marines</a> occupied Nicaragua from 1912 to 1933,<sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 111, 197">&#58;&#8202;111,&#8202;197&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> except for a nine-month period beginning in 1925. In 1914, the <a href="/wiki/Bryan%E2%80%93Chamorro_Treaty" title="Bryan–Chamorro Treaty">Bryan–Chamorro Treaty</a> was signed, giving the U.S. control over a proposed canal through Nicaragua, as well as leases for potential canal defenses.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> After the U.S. Marines left, another <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_civil_war_(1926%E2%80%9327)" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan civil war (1926–27)">violent conflict</a> between Liberals and Conservatives in 1926, resulted in the return of U.S. Marines.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:General_Sandino_(center)_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.,_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif/lossy-page1-220px-General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif/lossy-page1-330px-General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif/lossy-page1-440px-General_Sandino_%28center%29_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.%2C_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1869" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:General_Sandino_(center)_and_Staff_enroute_to_Mexico._Siglo_XX.,_06-1929_-_NARA_-_532357.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Rebel leader <a href="/wiki/Augusto_C%C3%A9sar_Sandino" title="Augusto César Sandino">Augusto César Sandino</a> (center)</div></div></div>
<p>From 1927 to 1933, rebel general <a href="/wiki/Augusto_C%C3%A9sar_Sandino" title="Augusto César Sandino">Augusto César Sandino</a> led a sustained guerrilla war against the Conservative regime and then against the U.S. Marines, whom he fought for over five years.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> When the Americans left in 1933, they set up the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Guard_(Nicaragua)" title="National Guard (Nicaragua)">Guardia Nacional</a></i> (national guard),<sup id="cite_ref-SY_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SY-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> a combined military and police force trained and equipped by the Americans and designed to be loyal to U.S. interests.
</p><p>After the U.S. Marines withdrew from Nicaragua in January 1933, Sandino and the newly elected administration of President <a href="/wiki/Juan_Bautista_Sacasa" title="Juan Bautista Sacasa">Juan Bautista Sacasa</a> reached an agreement that Sandino would cease his guerrilla activities in return for amnesty, a land grant for an agricultural colony, and retention of an armed band of 100 men for a year.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> However, due to a growing hostility between Sandino and National Guard director <a href="/wiki/Anastasio_Somoza_Garc%C3%ADa" title="Anastasio Somoza García">Anastasio Somoza García</a> and a fear of armed opposition from Sandino, Somoza García ordered his assassination.<sup id="cite_ref-SY_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SY-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>  Sacasa invited Sandino for dinner and to sign a peace treaty at the Presidential House on the night of February 21, 1934. After leaving the Presidential House, Sandino's car was stopped by National Guard soldiers and they kidnapped him. Later that night, Sandino was assassinated by National Guard soldiers. Later, hundreds of men, women, and children from Sandino's agricultural colony were murdered.<sup id="cite_ref-SomozaDynasty_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SomozaDynasty-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Somoza_dynasty_.281927.E2.80.931979.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Somoza_dynasty_(1927–1979)">Somoza dynasty (1927–1979)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Somoza dynasty (1927–1979)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg/220px-Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="203" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg/330px-Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg/440px-Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg 2x" data-file-width="617" data-file-height="570" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Trujillo-Somoza_1952.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>President <a href="/wiki/Anastasio_Somoza_Garc%C3%ADa" title="Anastasio Somoza García">Anastasio Somoza García</a> (left), with <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic" title="Dominican Republic">Dominican</a> President <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo" title="Rafael Trujillo">Rafael Trujillo</a>, 1952</div></div></div>
<p>Nicaragua has experienced several military dictatorships, the longest being the hereditary dictatorship of the <a href="/wiki/Somoza_family" title="Somoza family">Somoza family</a>, who ruled for 43 nonconsecutive years during the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The Somoza family came to power as part of a U.S.-engineered pact in 1927 that stipulated the formation of the <i>Guardia Nacional</i> to replace the marines who had long reigned in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Somoza García slowly eliminated officers in the national guard who might have stood in his way, and then deposed Sacasa and became president on January 1, 1937, in a <a href="/wiki/Electoral_fraud" title="Electoral fraud">rigged election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SY_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SY-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1941, during the Second World War, Nicaragua declared war on <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japan</a> (8 December), <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Germany</a> (11 December), <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy" title="Kingdom of Italy">Italy</a> (11 December), <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bulgaria" title="Kingdom of Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a> (19 December), <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary_(1920%E2%80%931946)" title="Kingdom of Hungary (1920–1946)">Hungary</a> (19 December) and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania" title="Kingdom of Romania">Romania</a> (19 December). Only Romania reciprocated, declaring war on Nicaragua on the same day (19 December 1941).<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> No soldiers were sent to the war, but Somoza García confiscated properties held by <a href="/wiki/German_Nicaraguan" title="German Nicaraguan">German Nicaraguan</a> residents.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> In 1945, Nicaragua was among the first countries to ratify the United Nations Charter.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua,_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg/220px-Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="113" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg/330px-Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg/440px-Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua%2C_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1152" data-file-height="590" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Meeting_with_President_Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle_of_Nicaragua,_before_State_Dinner_-_NARA_-_194723-perspective-tilt-crop.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle" title="Anastasio Somoza Debayle">Anastasio Somoza Debayle</a> (center) with <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Richard Nixon</a>, 1971</div></div></div>
<p>On September 29, 1956,<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Somoza García was shot to death by <a href="/wiki/Rigoberto_L%C3%B3pez_P%C3%A9rez" title="Rigoberto López Pérez">Rigoberto López Pérez</a>, a 27-year-old Liberal Nicaraguan poet. <a href="/wiki/Luis_Somoza_Debayle" title="Luis Somoza Debayle">Luis Somoza Debayle</a>, the eldest son of the late president, was appointed president by the congress and officially took charge of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-SY_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SY-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> He is remembered by some as moderate, but after only a few years in power died of a heart attack. His successor as president was <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Schick_Guti%C3%A9rrez" class="mw-redirect" title="René Schick Gutiérrez">René Schick Gutiérrez</a>, whom most Nicaraguans viewed "as nothing more than a puppet of the Somozas".<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Somoza García's youngest son, <a href="/wiki/Anastasio_Somoza_Debayle" title="Anastasio Somoza Debayle">Anastasio Somoza Debayle</a>, often referred to simply as "Somoza", became president in 1967.
</p><p>An <a href="/wiki/1972_Nicaragua_earthquake" title="1972 Nicaragua earthquake">earthquake in 1972</a> destroyed nearly 90% of Managua, including much of its infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Instead of helping to rebuild the city, Somoza siphoned off relief money. The mishandling of relief money also prompted <a href="/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates" title="Pittsburgh Pirates">Pittsburgh Pirates</a> star <a href="/wiki/Roberto_Clemente" title="Roberto Clemente">Roberto Clemente</a> to personally fly to Managua on December 31, 1972, but he died <i>en route</i> in an airplane accident.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Even the economic elite were reluctant to support Somoza, as he had acquired monopolies in industries that were key to rebuilding the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Somoza family was among a few families or groups of influential firms which reaped most of the benefits of the country's growth from the 1950s to the 1970s. When Somoza was deposed by the Sandinistas in 1979, the family's worth was estimated to be between $500&#160;million and $1.5&#160;billion.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Nicaraguan_Revolution_.281960s.E2.80.931990.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Nicaraguan_Revolution_(1960s–1990)">Nicaraguan Revolution (1960s–1990)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Nicaraguan Revolution (1960s–1990)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution" title="Nicaraguan Revolution">Nicaraguan Revolution</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg/220px-Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg/330px-Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg/440px-Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1160" data-file-height="792" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Smoke_break_el_serrano_1987.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>United States–supported anti-Sandinista "<a href="/wiki/Contras" title="Contras">Contra</a>" rebels (ARDE Frente Sur) in 1987</div></div></div>
<p>In 1961, <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Fonseca" title="Carlos Fonseca">Carlos Fonseca</a> looked back to the historical figure of Sandino, and along with two other people (one of whom was believed to be Casimiro Sotelo, who was later assassinated), founded the <a href="/wiki/Sandinista_National_Liberation_Front" title="Sandinista National Liberation Front">Sandinista National Liberation Front</a> (FSLN).<sup id="cite_ref-SY_64-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SY-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> After the 1972 earthquake and Somoza's apparent corruption, the ranks of the Sandinistas were flooded with young disaffected Nicaraguans who no longer had anything to lose.<sup id="cite_ref-Grinnell2007_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grinnell2007-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 1974, a group of the FSLN, in an attempt to kidnap U.S. ambassador Turner Shelton, held some Managuan partygoers hostage (after killing the host, former agriculture minister, Jose Maria Castillo), until the Somozan government met their demands for a large ransom and free transport to Cuba. Somoza granted this, then subsequently sent his national guard out into the countryside to look for the kidnappers, described by opponents of the kidnapping as "terrorists".<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On January 10, 1978, <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Joaqu%C3%ADn_Chamorro_Cardenal" title="Pedro Joaquín Chamorro Cardenal">Pedro Joaquín Chamorro Cardenal</a>, the editor of the national newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/La_Prensa_(Managua)" title="La Prensa (Managua)">La Prensa</a></i> and ardent opponent of Somoza, was assassinated.<sup id="cite_ref-AC_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AC-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> It is alleged that the planners and perpetrators of the murder were at the highest echelons of the Somoza regime.<sup id="cite_ref-AC_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AC-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Sandinistas forcefully took power in July 1979, ousting Somoza, and prompting the exodus of the majority of Nicaragua's middle class, wealthy landowners, and professionals, many of whom settled in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The Carter administration decided to work with the new government, while attaching a provision for aid forfeiture if it was found to be assisting insurgencies in neighboring countries.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Somoza fled the country and eventually ended up in <a href="/wiki/Paraguay" title="Paraguay">Paraguay</a>, where he was assassinated in September 1980, allegedly by members of the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Argentinian_Revolutionary_Workers%27_Party&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Argentinian Revolutionary Workers&#39; Party (page does not exist)">Argentinian Revolutionary Workers' Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1980, the Carter administration provided $60&#160;million in aid to Nicaragua under the Sandinistas, but the aid was suspended when the administration obtained evidence of Nicaraguan shipment of arms to El Salvadoran rebels.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Most people sided with Nicaragua against the Sandinistas.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In response to the coming to power of the Sandinistas, various rebel groups collectively known as the "<a href="/wiki/Contras" title="Contras">Contras</a>" were formed to oppose the new government. The <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Reagan</a> administration authorized the <a href="/wiki/Central_Intelligence_Agency" title="Central Intelligence Agency">CIA</a> to <a href="/wiki/United_States_and_state-sponsored_terrorism" title="United States and state-sponsored terrorism">help the Contra rebels</a> with funding, weapons and training.<sup id="cite_ref-CA_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CA-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The Contras operated from camps in the neighboring countries of Honduras to the north and Costa Rica to the south.<sup id="cite_ref-CA_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CA-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua,_1989.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg/220px-10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg/330px-10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg/440px-10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua%2C_1989.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="421" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:10th_anniversary_of_the_Nicaraguan_revolution_in_Managua,_1989.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>10th anniversary of the Nicaraguan revolution in Managua, 1989</div></div></div>
<p>They engaged in a systematic campaign of terror among rural Nicaraguans to disrupt the social reform projects of the Sandinistas. Several historians have criticized the Contra campaign and the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration" title="Foreign policy of the Ronald Reagan administration">Reagan administration's support for the Contras</a>, citing the brutality and numerous human rights violations of the Contras. LaRamee and Polakoff, for example, describe the destruction of health centers, schools, and cooperatives at the hands of the rebels,<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> and others have contended that murder, rape, and torture occurred on a large scale in Contra-dominated areas.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. also carried out a campaign of economic sabotage, and disrupted shipping by planting underwater mines in Nicaragua's port of <a href="/wiki/Corinto,_Nicaragua" title="Corinto, Nicaragua">Corinto</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> an action <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua_v._United_States" title="Nicaragua v. United States">condemned</a> by the <a href="/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice" title="International Court of Justice">International Court of Justice</a> as illegal.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>  The court also found that the U.S. encouraged acts contrary to humanitarian law by producing the manual <i><a href="/wiki/Psychological_Operations_in_Guerrilla_Warfare" title="Psychological Operations in Guerrilla Warfare">Psychological Operations in Guerrilla Warfare</a></i> and disseminating it to the Contras.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The manual, among other things, advised on how to rationalize killings of civilians.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. also sought to place economic pressure on the Sandinistas, and the Reagan administration imposed a full trade embargo.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Sandinistas were also accused of human rights abuses including torture, disappearances and mass executions.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Inter-American_Commission_on_Human_Rights" title="Inter-American Commission on Human Rights">Inter-American Commission on Human Rights</a> investigated abuses by Sandinista forces, including an execution of 35 to 40 <a href="/wiki/Miskito_people" title="Miskito people">Miskitos</a> in December 1981,<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> and an execution of 75 people in November 1984.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_1984" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 1984">Nicaraguan general elections of 1984</a>, which were judged by at least one visiting 30-person delegation of NGO representatives to have been free and fair,<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> the Sandinistas won the parliamentary election and their leader <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Ortega" title="Daniel Ortega">Daniel Ortega</a> won the presidential election.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The Reagan administration criticized the elections as a "sham" based on the claim that <a href="/wiki/Arturo_Cruz" title="Arturo Cruz">Arturo Cruz</a>, the candidate nominated by the <a href="/wiki/Coordinadora_Democr%C3%A1tica_Nicarag%C3%BCense" title="Coordinadora Democrática Nicaragüense">Coordinadora Democrática Nicaragüense</a>, comprising three right wing political parties, did not participate in the elections. However, the administration privately argued against Cruz's participation for fear that his involvement would legitimize the elections, and thus weaken the case for American aid to the Contras.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> According to Martin Kriele, the results of the election were rigged.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1983 the U.S. Congress prohibited federal funding of the Contras, but the Reagan administration illegally continued to back them by covertly selling arms to <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Iran</a> and channeling the proceeds to the Contras in the <a href="/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair" title="Iran–Contra affair">Iran–Contra affair</a>, for which several members of the Reagan administration were convicted of felonies.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice" title="International Court of Justice">International Court of Justice</a>, in regard to the case of <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua_v._United_States" title="Nicaragua v. United States">Nicaragua v. United States</a> in 1986, found, "the United States of America was under an obligation to make reparation to the Republic of Nicaragua for all injury caused to Nicaragua by certain breaches of obligations under customary international law and treaty-law committed by the United States of America".<sup id="cite_ref-icj-cij_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icj-cij-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> During the war between the Contras and the Sandinistas, 30,000 people were killed.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Post-war_.281990.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-war_(1990–present)">Post-war (1990–present)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Post-war (1990–present)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Violeta_Chamorro_1993.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Violeta_Chamorro_1993.jpg/170px-Violeta_Chamorro_1993.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/35/Violeta_Chamorro_1993.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="253" data-file-height="336" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Violeta_Chamorro_1993.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Violeta_Chamorro" title="Violeta Chamorro">Violeta Chamorro</a> in 1990 became the first woman president democratically elected in the Americas.</div></div></div>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_1990" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 1990">Nicaraguan general election, 1990</a>, a coalition of anti-Sandinista parties (from the left and right of the political spectrum) led by <a href="/wiki/Violeta_Chamorro" title="Violeta Chamorro">Violeta Chamorro</a>, the widow of Pedro Joaquín Chamorro Cardenal, defeated the Sandinistas. The defeat shocked the Sandinistas, who had expected to win.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Exit polls of Nicaraguans reported Chamorro's victory over Ortega was achieved with a 55% majority.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Chamorro was the first woman president of Nicaragua. Ortega vowed he would govern <i>desde abajo</i> (from below).<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Chamorro came to office with an economy in ruins, primarily because of the financial and social costs of the Contra War with the Sandinista-led government.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> In the next election, the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_1996" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 1996">Nicaraguan general election, 1996</a>, Daniel Ortega and the Sandinistas of the FSLN lost again, this time to <a href="/wiki/Arnoldo_Alem%C3%A1n" title="Arnoldo Alemán">Arnoldo Alemán</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Constitutionalist_Liberal_Party_(Nicaragua)" class="mw-redirect" title="Constitutionalist Liberal Party (Nicaragua)">Constitutional Liberal Party</a> (PLC).
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mitch-Flooding_in_Managua.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Mitch-Flooding_in_Managua.jpg/220px-Mitch-Flooding_in_Managua.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b3/Mitch-Flooding_in_Managua.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="168" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mitch-Flooding_in_Managua.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Flooding in Lake Managua after <a href="/wiki/Hurricane_Mitch" title="Hurricane Mitch">Hurricane Mitch</a> in 1998</div></div></div>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_2001" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 2001">the 2001 elections</a>, the PLC again defeated the FSLN, with Alemán's Vice President <a href="/wiki/Enrique_Bola%C3%B1os" title="Enrique Bolaños">Enrique Bolaños</a> succeeding him as president. However, Alemán was convicted and sentenced in 2003 to 20 years in prison for <a href="/wiki/Embezzlement" title="Embezzlement">embezzlement</a>, <a href="/wiki/Money_laundering" title="Money laundering">money laundering</a>, and corruption;<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> liberal and Sandinista parliament members combined to strip the presidential powers of President Bolaños and his ministers, calling for his resignation and threatening <a href="/wiki/Impeachment" title="Impeachment">impeachment</a>. The Sandinistas said they no longer supported Bolaños after U.S. Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Colin_Powell" title="Colin Powell">Colin Powell</a> told Bolaños to distance from the FSLN.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> This "slow motion <i>coup d'état</i>" was averted partially by pressure from the Central American presidents, who vowed not to recognize any movement that removed Bolaños; the U.S., the OAS, and the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> also opposed the action.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_2006" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 2006">the general elections on November 5, 2006</a>, the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Nicaragua">National Assembly</a> passed a bill further restricting <a href="/wiki/Abortion_in_Nicaragua" title="Abortion in Nicaragua">abortion in Nicaragua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> As a result, Nicaragua is one of five countries in the world where abortion is illegal with no exceptions.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Legislative and presidential elections took place on November 5, 2006. Ortega returned to the presidency with 37.99% of the vote. This percentage was enough to win the presidency outright, because of a change in electoral law which lowered the percentage requiring a runoff election from 45% to 35% (with a 5% margin of victory).<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Nicaragua's 2011 general election resulted in re-election of Ortega, with a landslide victory and 62.46% of the vote. In 2014 the National Assembly approved changes to the constitution allowing Ortega to run for a third successive term.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2016, <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_general_election,_2016" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan general election, 2016">Ortega was elected for his third consecutive term</a> (his fourth overall). International monitoring of the elections was initially prohibited, and as a result the validity of the <a href="/wiki/Elections_in_Nicaragua" title="Elections in Nicaragua">elections</a> has been disputed, but observation by the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">OAS</a> was announced in October.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Ortega was reported by Nicaraguan election officials as having received 72% of the vote. However the <a href="/wiki/Broad_Front_for_Democracy" title="Broad Front for Democracy">Broad Front for Democracy</a> (FAD), having promoted boycotts of the elections, claimed that 70% of voters had abstained (while election officials claimed 65.8% participation).<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2018, <a href="/wiki/2018%E2%80%932020_Nicaraguan_protests" class="mw-redirect" title="2018–2020 Nicaraguan protests">demonstrations</a> opposed a decree increasing taxes and reducing benefits in the country's pension system. Local independent press organizations had documented at least 19 dead and over 100 missing in the ensuing conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> A reporter from NPR spoke to protestors who explained that while the initial issue was about the pension reform, the uprisings that spread across the country reflected many grievances about the government's time in office, and that the fight is for President Ortega and his vice president wife to step down.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> April 24, 2018 marked the day of the greatest march in opposition of the Sandinista party. On May 2, 2018, university-student leaders publicly announced that they give the government seven days to set a date and time for a dialogue that was promised to the people due to the recent events of repression. The students also scheduled another march on that same day for a peaceful protest. As of May 2018, estimates of the death toll were as high as 63, many of them student protesters, and the wounded totalled more than 400.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Following a working visit from May 17 to 21, the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights adopted precautionary measures aimed at protecting members of the student movement and their families after testimonies indicated the majority of them had suffered acts of violence and death threats for their participation.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> In the last week of May, thousands who accuse Mr. Ortega and his wife of acting like dictators joined in resuming anti-government rallies after attempted peace talks have remained unresolved.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography_and_climate">Geography and climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Geography and climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Nicaragua" title="Geography of Nicaragua">Geography of Nicaragua</a> and <a href="/wiki/Climate_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Climate of Nicaragua">Climate of Nicaragua</a></div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Volcanoes_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Volcanoes of Nicaragua">Volcanoes of Nicaragua</a></div>
