<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_boys.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Nicaragua_boys.jpg/220px-Nicaragua_boys.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Nicaragua_boys.jpg/330px-Nicaragua_boys.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Nicaragua_boys.jpg/440px-Nicaragua_boys.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="388" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_boys.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicaraguan High school students at the <a href="/w/index.php?title=American_Nicaraguan_School&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="American Nicaraguan School (page does not exist)">American Nicaraguan School</a></div></div></div><p>According to a 2014 research published in the journal <i>Genetics and Molecular Biology</i>, European ancestry predominates in 69% of Nicaraguans, followed by African ancestry in 20%, and lastly <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">indigenous ancestry</a> in 11%.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> A Japanese research of "Genomic Components in America's demography" demonstrated that, on average, the ancestry of Nicaraguans is 58–62% European, 28% Native American, and 14% African, with a very small Near Eastern contribution.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Non-genetic data from the <i><a href="/wiki/CIA_World_Factbook" class="mw-redirect" title="CIA World Factbook">CIA World Factbook</a></i> establish that from Nicaragua's 2016 population of 5,966,798, around 69% are <a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">mestizo</a>, 17% <a href="/wiki/White_Latin_American" class="mw-redirect" title="White Latin American">white</a>, 5% Native American, and 9% <a href="/wiki/Afro-Latin_American" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-Latin American">black</a> and other races.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_6-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> This fluctuates with changes in migration patterns. The population is 58% urban as of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The capital <a href="/wiki/Managua" title="Managua">Managua</a> is the biggest city, with an estimated population of 1,042,641 in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> In 2005, over 5&#160;million people lived in the Pacific, Central and North regions, and 700,000 in the Caribbean region.<sup id="cite_ref-CN_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CN-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There is a growing expatriate community,<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> the majority of whom move for business, investment or retirement from across the world, such as from the US, Canada, <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a>, and European countries; the majority have settled in Managua, <a href="/wiki/Granada,_Nicaragua" title="Granada, Nicaragua">Granada</a> and <a href="/wiki/San_Juan_del_Sur" title="San Juan del Sur">San Juan del Sur</a>.
</p><p>Many <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Diaspora" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan Diaspora">Nicaraguans live abroad</a>, particularly in Costa Rica, the United States, Spain, Canada, and other Central American countries.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicaragua has a population growth rate of 1.5% as of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> This is the result of one of the highest <a href="/wiki/Birth_rate" title="Birth rate">birth rates</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a>:<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="Is this still correct as of 2017? (January 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> 17.7 per 1,000 as of 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> The death rate was 4.7 per 1,000 during the same period according to the United Nations.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Ethnic groups">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:AfroNicoya.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/AfroNicoya.jpg/170px-AfroNicoya.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/AfroNicoya.jpg/255px-AfroNicoya.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a1/AfroNicoya.jpg 2x" data-file-width="315" data-file-height="418" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:AfroNicoya.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Afro-Nicaraguan</div></div></div>
<p>The majority of the Nicaraguan population is composed of mestizos, roughly 69%, while 17% of Nicaragua's population is white,<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> with the majority of them being of Spanish descent, while others are of German, Italian, English, Turkish, Danish or French ancestry.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Black_Creoles">Black Creoles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Black Creoles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>About 9% of Nicaragua's population is <a href="/wiki/Afro-Latin_American" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-Latin American">black</a> and mainly resides on the country's Caribbean (or Atlantic) coast. The black population is mostly composed of black English-speaking Creoles who are the descendants of escaped or shipwrecked slaves; many carry the name of <a href="/wiki/Scottish_settlers" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish settlers">Scottish settlers</a> who brought slaves with them, such as <a href="/wiki/Campbell_(surname)" title="Campbell (surname)">Campbell</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gordon_(surname)" title="Gordon (surname)">Gordon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Downs_(surname)" title="Downs (surname)">Downs</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hodgson" title="Hodgson">Hodgson</a>. Although many Creoles supported Somoza because of his close association with the United States, they rallied to the Sandinista cause in July 1979, only to reject the revolution soon afterwards in response to a new phase of "westernization" and imposition of central rule from Managua.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> There is a smaller number of <a href="/wiki/Garifuna_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Garifuna people">Garifuna</a>, a people of mixed <a href="/wiki/West_African" class="mw-redirect" title="West African">West African</a>, <a href="/wiki/Island_Carib" class="mw-redirect" title="Island Carib">Carib</a> and <a href="/wiki/Arawak" title="Arawak">Arawak</a> descent. In the mid-1980s, the government divided the <a href="/wiki/Zelaya_Department" title="Zelaya Department">Zelaya Department</a> – consisting of the eastern half of the country – into two autonomous regions and granted the black and indigenous people of this region limited self-rule within the republic.
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Amerindians"></span>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Indigenous_population">Indigenous population</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Indigenous population">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The remaining 5% of Nicaraguans are indigenous, the descendants of the country's original inhabitants. Nicaragua's <a href="/wiki/Pre-Columbian" class="mw-redirect" title="Pre-Columbian">pre-Columbian</a> population consisted of many indigenous groups. In the western region, the Nahuas (<a href="/wiki/Nicarao_people" title="Nicarao people">Nicarao people</a>) were present along with other groups such as the Chorotega people and the <a href="/wiki/Subtiaba" class="mw-redirect" title="Subtiaba">Subtiabas</a> (also known as Maribios or Hokan Xiu). The central region and the Caribbean coast of Nicaragua were inhabited by indigenous peoples who were <a href="/wiki/Macro-Chibchan_languages" title="Macro-Chibchan languages">Macro-Chibchan language</a> groups that had migrated to and from South America in ancient times, primarily what is now <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuela</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> These groups include the present-day <a href="/wiki/Cacaopera_people" title="Cacaopera people">Matagalpas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Miskito_people" title="Miskito people">Miskitos</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rama_people" title="Rama people">Ramas</a>, as well as Mayangnas and Ulwas who are also known as <a href="/wiki/Sumo_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Sumo people">Sumos</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Brief_30-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brief-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 20">&#58;&#8202;20&#8202;</span></sup> In the 19th century, there was a substantial indigenous minority, but this group was largely assimilated culturally into the mestizo majority. The <a href="/wiki/Garifuna" title="Garifuna">Garifuna</a> are also present, mainly on the Caribbean Coast. They are a people of mixed African and Indigenous descent.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Nicaragua" title="Languages of Nicaragua">Languages of Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:BluefieldsSign.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/BluefieldsSign.jpg/220px-BluefieldsSign.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/BluefieldsSign.jpg/330px-BluefieldsSign.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/BluefieldsSign.jpg/440px-BluefieldsSign.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="737" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:BluefieldsSign.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A sign in Bluefields in English (top), Spanish (middle) and Miskito (bottom)</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Spanish" title="Nicaraguan Spanish">Nicaraguan Spanish</a> has many indigenous influences and several distinguishing characteristics. For example, some Nicaraguans have a tendency to replace <a href="/wiki/Voiceless_alveolar_sibilant" class="mw-redirect" title="Voiceless alveolar sibilant">/s/</a> with <a href="/wiki/Voiceless_glottal_fricative" title="Voiceless glottal fricative">/h/</a> when speaking.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Although Spanish is spoken throughout, the country has great variety: vocabulary, accents and colloquial language can vary between towns and departments.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Sign_Language" title="Nicaraguan Sign Language">Nicaraguan Sign Language</a> emerged in the 1970s and 1980s among deaf children as the first special education schools brought them together, and its emergence became of particular interest to linguists as an opportunity to directly observe the creation of a language.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the Caribbean coast, indigenous languages, <a href="/wiki/English-based_creole_languages" title="English-based creole languages">English-based creoles</a>, and Spanish are spoken. The <a href="/wiki/Miskito_language" title="Miskito language">Miskito language</a>, spoken by the <a href="/wiki/Miskito_people" title="Miskito people">Miskito people</a> as a first language and some other indigenous and Afro-descendants people as a second, third, or fourth language, is the most commonly spoken indigenous language. The indigenous <a href="/wiki/Misumalpan_languages" title="Misumalpan languages">Misumalpan languages</a> of Mayangna and Ulwa are spoken by the respective peoples of the same names. Many Miskito, Mayangna, and Sumo people also speak <a href="/wiki/Miskito_Coast_Creole" title="Miskito Coast Creole">Miskito Coast Creole</a>, and a large majority also speak Spanish. Fewer than three dozen of nearly 2,000 <a href="/wiki/Rama_people" title="Rama people">Rama people</a> speak their <a href="/wiki/Chibchan_languages" title="Chibchan languages">Chibchan</a> <a href="/wiki/Rama_language" title="Rama language">language</a> fluently, with nearly all Ramas speaking <a href="/wiki/Rama_Cay_Creole" title="Rama Cay Creole">Rama Cay Creole</a> and the vast majority speaking Spanish. Linguists have attempted to document and revitalize the language over the past three decades.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Garifuna_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Garifuna people">Garifuna people</a>, descendants of indigenous and Afro-descendant people who came to Nicaragua from Honduras in the early twentieth century, have recently attempted to revitalize their <a href="/wiki/Arawakan_languages" title="Arawakan languages">Arawakan</a> <a href="/wiki/Garifuna_language" title="Garifuna language">language</a>. The majority speak Miskito Coast Creole as their first language and Spanish as their second. The Creole or Kriol people, descendants of enslaved Africans brought to the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> during the British colonial period and European, Chinese, Arab, and British West Indian immigrants, also speak Miskito Coast Creole as their first language and Spanish as their second.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Nicaragua" title="Religion in Nicaragua">Religion in Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG/220px-Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG/330px-Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG/440px-Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="960" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kathedrale_Leon_2.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Le%C3%B3n_Cathedral_(Nicaragua)" class="mw-redirect" title="León Cathedral (Nicaragua)">León Cathedral</a>, one of Nicaragua's <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">World Heritage Sites</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Religion plays a significant part of the culture of Nicaragua and is afforded special protections in the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Nicaragua" title="Constitution of Nicaragua">constitution</a>. Religious freedom, which has been guaranteed since 1939, and religious tolerance are officially promoted by the government and the constitution, but in recent years the Catholic Church and the regime led by <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Ortega" title="Daniel Ortega">Daniel Ortega</a> have been in open conflict, with the latter being accused of using the police to harass clergy and bishops,<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> closing down Catholic media outlets, and even arresting a dozen members of the clergy, including <a href="/wiki/Rolando_Jos%C3%A9_%C3%81lvarez_Lagos" title="Rolando José Álvarez Lagos">Bishop Rolando Alvarez</a>, of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Matagalpa" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Matagalpa">Diocese of Matagalpa</a>.
</p><p>Nicaragua has no official religion. Catholic bishops are expected to lend their authority to important state occasions, and their pronouncements on national issues are closely followed. They can be called upon to mediate between contending parties at moments of political crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-LOCR_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOCR-234">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> In 1979, Miguel D'Escoto Brockman, a priest who had embraced <a href="/wiki/Liberation_Theology" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberation Theology">Liberation Theology</a>, served in the government as foreign minister when the Sandinistas came to power. The largest denomination, and traditionally the religion of the majority, is the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>. It came to Nicaragua in the 16th century with the Spanish conquest and remained, until 1939, the <a href="/wiki/State_religion" title="State religion">established faith</a>.
</p><p>The number of practicing Roman Catholics has been declining, while membership of <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Protestant">evangelical Protestant</a> groups and <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> (LDS Church) has been growing rapidly since the 1990s. There is a significant LDS missionary effort in Nicaragua. There are two missions and 95,768 members of the LDS Church (1.54% of the population).<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> There are also strong <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglican</a> and <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravian</a> communities on the Caribbean coast in what once constituted the sparsely populated <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> colony. It was under British influence for nearly three centuries. <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a> was brought to the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> mainly by British and German colonists in forms of <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravian Church</a>. Other kinds of Protestant and other <a href="/wiki/Christian_denomination" title="Christian denomination">Christian denominations</a> were introduced to the rest of Nicaragua during the 19th century.
</p><p>Popular religion revolves around the saints, who are perceived as intercessors between human beings and God. Most localities, from the capital of Managua to small rural communities, honor <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a>, selected from the Roman Catholic calendar, with annual <i>fiestas</i>. In many communities, a rich lore has grown up around the celebrations of patron saints, such as Managua's Saint Dominic (Santo Domingo), honored in August with two colorful, often riotous, day-long processions through the city. The high point of Nicaragua's religious calendar for the masses is neither Christmas nor Easter, but La Purísima, a week of festivities in early December dedicated to the <a href="/wiki/Immaculate_Conception" title="Immaculate Conception">Immaculate Conception</a>, during which elaborate altars to the <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Virgin Mary</a> are constructed in homes and workplaces.<sup id="cite_ref-LOCR_234-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOCR-234">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a> has increased with a steady influx of immigration.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> have been living in Nicaragua since the 18th century, the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Nicaragua" title="History of the Jews in Nicaragua">Jewish population</a> is small, numbering less than 200 people in 2017.  Of these, 112 were recent converts who claimed  <a href="/wiki/Sephardi_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Sephardi Jews">Sephardic</a> Jewish ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-Dolsten_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dolsten-237">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As of 2007, approximately 1,200 to 1,500 Nicaraguan residents practiced <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Nicaragua" title="Islam in Nicaragua">Islam</a>, most of them <a href="/wiki/Sunnis" class="mw-redirect" title="Sunnis">Sunnis</a> who are <a href="/wiki/Resident_aliens" class="mw-redirect" title="Resident aliens">resident aliens</a> or <a href="/wiki/Naturalized" class="mw-redirect" title="Naturalized">naturalized citizens</a> from <a href="/wiki/State_of_Palestine" title="State of Palestine">Palestine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Iran</a> or natural-born Nicaraguan descendants of the two groups.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Immigration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Nicaragua" title="Immigration to Nicaragua">Immigration to Nicaragua</a></div>
<p>Relative to its population, Nicaragua has not experienced large waves of immigration. The number of immigrants in Nicaragua, from other Latin American countries or other countries, never surpassed 1% of its total population before 1995. The 2005 census showed the foreign-born population at 1.2%, having risen a mere 0.06% in 10 years.<sup id="cite_ref-CN_214-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CN-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 19th century, Nicaragua experienced modest waves of immigration from Europe. In particular, families from Germany, Italy, Spain, France and Belgium immigrated to Nicaragua, particularly the departments in the Central and Pacific region.
</p><p>Also present is a small Middle Eastern-Nicaraguan community of <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Syria" title="Demographics of Syria">Syrians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Armenians" title="Armenians">Armenians</a>, <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="History of the Jews of Nicaragua">Jewish Nicaraguans</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanese</a> people in Nicaragua. This community numbers about 30,000. There is an East Asian community mostly consisting of <a href="/wiki/Han_Chinese" title="Han Chinese">Chinese</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Nicaraguan" class="mw-redirect" title="Chinese Nicaraguan">Chinese Nicaraguan</a> population is estimated at 12,000.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> The Chinese arrived in the late 19th century but were unsubstantiated until the 1920s.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Diaspora">Diaspora</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Diaspora">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_diaspora" title="Nicaraguan diaspora">Nicaraguan diaspora</a></div>
<p>The Civil War forced many Nicaraguans to start lives outside of their country. Many people emigrated during the 1990s and the first decade of the 21st century due to the lack of employment opportunities and poverty. The majority of the <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_Diaspora" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaraguan Diaspora">Nicaraguan Diaspora</a> migrated to the United States and <a href="/wiki/Costa_Rica" title="Costa Rica">Costa Rica</a>. Today one in six Nicaraguans live in these two countries.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The diaspora has seen Nicaraguans settling around in smaller communities in other parts of the world, particularly Western Europe. Small communities of Nicaraguans are found in France, Germany, Italy, Spain, Norway, Sweden and the United Kingdom. Communities also exist in Australia and New Zealand. Canada, Brazil and Argentina host small groups of these communities. In Asia, Japan hosts a small Nicaraguan community.
</p><p>Due to extreme poverty at home, many Nicaraguans are now living and working in neighboring <a href="/wiki/El_Salvador" title="El Salvador">El Salvador</a>, a country that has the <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">US dollar</a> as its currency.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Healthcare">Healthcare</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Healthcare">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Healthcare_in_Nicaragua" title="Healthcare in Nicaragua">Healthcare in Nicaragua</a></div><p>Although Nicaragua's health outcomes have improved over the past few decades with the efficient utilization of resources relative to other Central American nations, <a href="/wiki/Healthcare_in_Nicaragua" title="Healthcare in Nicaragua">healthcare in Nicaragua</a> still confronts challenges responding to its populations' diverse healthcare needs.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Nicaragua" title="Government of Nicaragua">Nicaraguan government</a> guarantees universal free <a href="/wiki/Health_care" title="Health care">health care</a> for its citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-244">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> However, limitations of current delivery models and unequal distribution of resources and medical personnel contribute to the persistent lack of quality care in more remote areas of Nicaragua, especially among rural communities in the Central and Atlantic region.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_243-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> To respond to the dynamic needs of localities, the government has adopted a decentralized model that emphasizes community-based preventive and primary medical care.<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Nicaragua" title="Education in Nicaragua">Education in Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa,_Managua.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg/220px-Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="108" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg/330px-Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg/440px-Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa%2C_Managua.jpg 2x" data-file-width="583" data-file-height="285" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Universidad_Nacional_De_Ingenier%C3%ADa,_Managua.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Universidad Nacional De Ingeniería "National University of Engineering", <a href="/wiki/Managua" title="Managua">Managua</a></div></div></div>
<p>The adult literacy rate in 2005 was 78.0%.<sup id="cite_ref-unescolit_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unescolit-246">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Primary education is free in Nicaragua. A system of private schools exists, many of which are religiously affiliated and often have more robust English programs.<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> As of 1979, the educational system was one of the poorest in Latin America.<sup id="cite_ref-NE_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NE-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> One of the first acts of the newly elected Sandinista government in 1980 was an extensive and successful literacy campaign, using secondary school students, university students and teachers as volunteer teachers: it reduced the overall illiteracy rate from 50.3% to 12.9% within only five months.<sup id="cite_ref-NLC_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NLC-249">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> This was one of a number of large-scale programs which received international recognition for their gains in <a href="/wiki/Literacy" title="Literacy">literacy</a>, health care, education, <a href="/wiki/Childcare" class="mw-redirect" title="Childcare">childcare</a>, <a href="/wiki/Trade_union" title="Trade union">unions</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Land_reform" title="Land reform">land reform</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> The Sandinistas also added a leftist ideological content to the curriculum, which was removed after 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-gr1_143-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gr1-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> In September 1980, <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> awarded Nicaragua the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> sponsored <a href="/wiki/UNESCO_Nadezhda_K._Krupskaya_literacy_prize" title="UNESCO Nadezhda K. Krupskaya literacy prize">Nadezhda Krupskaya award</a> for the literacy campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gender_equality">Gender equality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Gender equality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gender_equality_in_Nicaragua" title="Gender equality in Nicaragua">Gender equality in Nicaragua</a></div>
<p>Nicaragua's gender equality ranks high among countries in <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:02_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> When it came to global rankings regarding gender equality, the World Economic Forum ranked Nicaragua at number twelve in 2015,<sup id="cite_ref-:02_253-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> and in its 2020 report Nicaragua ranked number five, behind only northern European countries.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicaragua was among the many countries in Latin America and the Caribbean to ratify the <a href="/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women" title="Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women">Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women</a>, which aimed to promote women's rights.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2009, a Special Ombudsman for Sexual Diversity position was created within its Office of the Human Rights Ombudsman. And, in 2014, the Health Ministry in 2014 banned discrimination based on gender identity and sexual orientation.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup>  Nevertheless, discrimination against LGBTQ individuals is common, particularly in housing, education, and the workplace.<sup id="cite_ref-Nicaragua_162-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nicaragua-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Report" title="Human Development Report">The Human Development Report</a> ranked Nicaragua 106 out of 160 countries in the <a href="/wiki/Gender_Inequality_Index" title="Gender Inequality Index">Gender Inequality Index (GII)</a> in 2017. It reflects gender-based inequalities in three dimensions - reproductive health, empowerment, and economic activity.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Nicaragua" title="Culture of Nicaragua">Culture of Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg/220px-El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg/330px-El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg/440px-El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg 2x" data-file-width="820" data-file-height="616" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense" title="El Güegüense">El Güegüense</a> is a <a href="/wiki/Drama" title="Drama">drama</a> and was the first literary work of post-Columbian Nicaragua. It is regarded as one of <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>'s most distinctive colonial-era expressions and as Nicaragua's signature folkloric masterpiece combining music, dance and theatre.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Nicaragua" title="Culture of Nicaragua">Nicaraguan culture</a> has strong folklore, music and religious traditions, deeply influenced by <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Europe" title="Culture of Europe">European culture</a> but also including Native American sounds and flavors. Nicaraguan culture can further be defined in several distinct strands. The Pacific coast has strong folklore, music and religious traditions, deeply influenced by <a href="/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula" title="Iberian Peninsula">Europeans</a>. It was colonized by Spain and has a similar culture to other Spanish-speaking Latin American countries. The indigenous groups that historically inhabited the Pacific coast have largely been assimilated into the <a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">mestizo</a> culture.
</p><p>The Caribbean coast of Nicaragua was once a British protectorate. English is still predominant in this region and spoken domestically along with Spanish and <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Nicaragua" title="Languages of Nicaragua">indigenous languages</a>. Its culture is similar to that of Caribbean nations that were or are British possessions, such as <a href="/wiki/Jamaica" title="Jamaica">Jamaica</a>, Belize, the <a href="/wiki/Cayman_Islands" title="Cayman Islands">Cayman Islands</a>, etc. Unlike on the west coast, the indigenous peoples of the Caribbean coast have maintained distinct identities, and some still speak their native languages as first languages.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Nicaragua" title="Music of Nicaragua">Music of Nicaragua</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Music_of_Nicaragua" title="Music of Nicaragua">Nicaraguan music</a> is a mixture of indigenous and Spanish influences. Musical instruments include the <a href="/wiki/Marimba" title="Marimba">marimba</a> and others common across Central America. The marimba of Nicaragua is played by a sitting performer holding the instrument on his knees. He is usually accompanied by a bass <a href="/wiki/Fiddle" title="Fiddle">fiddle</a>, guitar and guitarrilla (a small guitar like a <a href="/wiki/Mandolin" title="Mandolin">mandolin</a>). This music is played at social functions as a sort of background music.
</p><p>The marimba is made with hardwood plates placed over bamboo or metal tubes of varying lengths. It is played with two or four <a href="/wiki/Hammer" title="Hammer">hammers</a>. The Caribbean coast of Nicaragua is known for a lively, sensual form of <a href="/wiki/Dance_music" title="Dance music">dance music</a> called <i><a href="/wiki/Palo_de_Mayo" title="Palo de Mayo">Palo de Mayo</a></i> which is popular throughout the country. It is especially loud and celebrated during the Palo de Mayo festival in May. The <a href="/wiki/Garifuna_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Garifuna people">Garifuna</a> community (Afro-Native American) is known for its popular music called <i><a href="/wiki/Punta_(music)" class="mw-redirect" title="Punta (music)">Punta</a></i>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg/170px-Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="308" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg/255px-Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg/340px-Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="1087" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicaragua_Mestizaje.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicaraguan women wearing the <a href="/wiki/Mestizaje" class="mw-redirect" title="Mestizaje">Mestizaje</a> costume, which is a traditional costume worn to dance the Mestizaje dance. The costume demonstrates the Spanish influence upon Nicaraguan clothing.<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Nicaragua enjoys a variety of international influence in the music arena. <a href="/wiki/Bachata_(music)" title="Bachata (music)">Bachata</a>, <a href="/wiki/Merengue_music" title="Merengue music">Merengue</a>, <a href="/wiki/Salsa_music" title="Salsa music">Salsa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cumbia_music" class="mw-redirect" title="Cumbia music">Cumbia</a> have gained prominence in cultural centres such as Managua, Leon and <a href="/wiki/Granada" title="Granada">Granada</a>. Cumbia dancing has grown popular with the introduction of Nicaraguan artists, including Gustavo Leyton, on <a href="/wiki/Ometepe_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Ometepe Island">Ometepe Island</a> and in Managua. <a href="/wiki/Salsa_dancing" class="mw-redirect" title="Salsa dancing">Salsa dancing</a> has become extremely popular in Managua's nightclubs. With various influences, the form of salsa dancing varies in Nicaragua. New York style and Cuban Salsa (Salsa Casino) elements have gained popularity across the country.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dance">Dance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Dance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Dance_in_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Dance in Nicaragua">Dance in Nicaragua</a></div>
<p>Dance in Nicaragua varies depending upon the region. Rural areas tend to have a stronger focus on movement of the hips and turns. The dance style in cities focuses primarily on more sophisticated footwork in addition to movement and turns. Combinations of styles from the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic" title="Dominican Republic">Dominican Republic</a> and the United States can be found throughout Nicaragua. <a href="/wiki/Bachata_(dance)" title="Bachata (dance)">Bachata dancing</a> is popular in Nicaragua. A considerable amount of Bachata dancing influence comes from Nicaraguans living abroad, in cities that include Miami, Los Angeles and, to a much lesser extent, New York City. <a href="/wiki/Tango_(dance)" class="mw-redirect" title="Tango (dance)">Tango</a> has also surfaced recently in cultural cities and ballroom dance occasions.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Literature_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Literature of Nicaragua">Literature of Nicaragua</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg/170px-Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg/255px-Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/72/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="291" data-file-height="425" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo" title="Rubén Darío">Rubén Darío</a>, the founder of the <a href="/wiki/Modernismo" title="Modernismo">modernismo</a> literary movement in Latin America</div></div></div>
<p>The origin of <a href="/wiki/Literature_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Literature of Nicaragua">Nicaraguan literature</a> can arguably be traced to <a href="/wiki/Pre-Columbian" class="mw-redirect" title="Pre-Columbian">pre-Columbian</a> times. The myths and <a href="/wiki/Oral_literature" title="Oral literature">oral literature</a> formed the cosmogenic view of the world of the indigenous people. Some of these stories are still known in Nicaragua. Like many Latin American countries, the Spanish conquerors have had the most effect on both the culture and the literature. Nicaraguan literature has historically been an important source of poetry in the Spanish-speaking world, with internationally renowned contributors such as <a href="/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo" title="Rubén Darío">Rubén Darío</a> who is regarded as the most important literary figure in Nicaragua. He is called the "Father of Modernism" for leading the <i><a href="/wiki/Modernismo" title="Modernismo">modernismo</a></i> literary movement at the end of the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-NI_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NI-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> Other literary figures include <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Martinez_Rivas" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlos Martinez Rivas">Carlos Martinez Rivas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pablo_Antonio_Cuadra" title="Pablo Antonio Cuadra">Pablo Antonio Cuadra</a>, Alberto Cuadra Mejia, Manolo Cuadra, Pablo Alberto Cuadra Arguello, Orlando Cuadra Downing, Alfredo Alegría Rosales, <a href="/wiki/Sergio_Ramirez_Mercado" class="mw-redirect" title="Sergio Ramirez Mercado">Sergio Ramirez Mercado</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ernesto_Cardenal" title="Ernesto Cardenal">Ernesto Cardenal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gioconda_Belli" title="Gioconda Belli">Gioconda Belli</a>, <a href="/wiki/Claribel_Alegr%C3%ADa" title="Claribel Alegría">Claribel Alegría</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Coronel_Urtecho" title="José Coronel Urtecho">José Coronel Urtecho</a>, among others.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The satirical drama <i><a href="/wiki/El_G%C3%BCeg%C3%BCense" title="El Güegüense">El Güegüense</a></i> was the first literary work of post-Columbian Nicaragua. It was written in both <a href="/wiki/Nicarao_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicarao language">Nicarao</a> and Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-Campbell1997_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Campbell1997-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 21">&#58;&#8202;21&#8202;</span></sup> It's regarded as one of Latin America's most distinctive colonial-era expressions and as Nicaragua's signature folkloric masterpiece. El Güegüense is a work of resistance to Spanish colonialism that combined music, dance and theatre.<sup id="cite_ref-NI_259-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NI-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Theatre" title="Theatre">theatrical</a> play was written by an anonymous author in the 16th century, making it one of the oldest <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Indigenous culture">indigenous</a> theatrical/dance works of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a>. In 2005 it was recognized by <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> as "a patrimony of humanity".<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> After centuries of popular performance, the play was first published in a book in 1942.<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Cuisine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nicaraguan_cuisine" title="Nicaraguan cuisine">Nicaraguan cuisine</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:VIGORON.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/VIGORON.JPG/220px-VIGORON.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/VIGORON.JPG/330px-VIGORON.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/VIGORON.JPG/440px-VIGORON.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:VIGORON.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Vigor%C3%B3n" title="Vigorón">Vigorón</a> is a dish that is served with boiled yuca and chicharrones (fried pork with skin) and topped with a cabbage salad.</div></div></div>
<p>Nicaraguan cuisine is a mixture of Spanish food and dishes of a pre-Columbian origin.<sup id="cite_ref-NICA_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NICA-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> Traditional cuisine changes from the Pacific to the Caribbean coast. The Pacific coast's main staple revolves around local fruits and corn, the Caribbean coast cuisine makes use of seafood and the coconut.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg/220px-Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg/330px-Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg/440px-Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3888" data-file-height="2592" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Costa_Rican_Gallo_Pinto.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Gallo_pinto" title="Gallo pinto">Gallo pinto</a> is a traditional dish of Nicaragua made with rice and beans.</div></div></div>
<p>As in many other Latin American countries, <a href="/wiki/Maize" title="Maize">maize</a> is a <a href="/wiki/Staple_food" title="Staple food">staple food</a> and is used in many of the widely consumed dishes, such as the <a href="/wiki/Nacatamal" title="Nacatamal">nacatamal</a>, <a href="/wiki/G%C3%BCirila" title="Güirila">güirila</a>, and <i><a href="/wiki/Indio_viejo" title="Indio viejo">indio viejo</a></i>. Maize is also an ingredient for drinks such as <a href="/wiki/Pinolillo" title="Pinolillo">pinolillo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chicha" title="Chicha">chicha</a> as well as sweets and desserts. In addition to corn, rice and beans are eaten very often.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Gallo_pinto" title="Gallo pinto">Gallo pinto</a>, Nicaragua's <a href="/wiki/National_dish" title="National dish">national dish</a>, is made with white rice and small red beans that are cooked individually and then fried together. The dish has several variations including the addition of <a href="/wiki/Coconut_milk" title="Coconut milk">coconut milk</a> and/or grated <a href="/wiki/Coconut" title="Coconut">coconut</a> on the Caribbean coast. Most Nicaraguans begin their day with gallo pinto. Gallo pinto is most usually served with <i>carne asada</i>, a salad, fried cheese, <a href="/wiki/Cooking_bananas" class="mw-redirect" title="Cooking bananas">plantains</a> or maduros.
</p><p>Many of Nicaragua's dishes include indigenous fruits and vegetables such as <a href="/wiki/Jocote" class="mw-redirect" title="Jocote">jocote</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mango" title="Mango">mango</a>, <a href="/wiki/Papaya" title="Papaya">papaya</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tamarind" title="Tamarind">tamarindo</a>, pipian, banana, <a href="/wiki/Avocado" title="Avocado">avocado</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cassava" title="Cassava">yuca</a>, and herbs such as <a href="/wiki/Coriander" title="Coriander">cilantro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oregano" title="Oregano">oregano</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bixa_orellana" title="Bixa orellana">achiote</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-NICA_263-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NICA-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Traditional street food snacks found in Nicaragua include "<a href="/wiki/Quesillo" title="Quesillo">quesillo</a>", a thick tortilla with soft cheese and cream, "tajadas" (deep-fried plantain chips), "maduros" (a sautéed ripe plantain), and "fresco" (fresh juices such as hibiscus and tamarind commonly served in a plastic bag with a straw).<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicaraguans have been known to eat <a href="/wiki/Guinea_pig" title="Guinea pig">guinea pigs</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> known as <i>cuy</i>. Tapirs, iguanas, turtle eggs, armadillos and boas are also sometimes eaten, but because of extinction threats to these wild creatures, there are efforts to curb this custom.<sup id="cite_ref-NICA_263-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NICA-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Media_of_Nicaragua" class="mw-redirect" title="Media of Nicaragua">Media of Nicaragua</a></div>
<p>For most Nicaraguans radio and TV are the main sources of news. There are more than 100 radio stations and several TV networks. Cable TV is available in most urban areas.<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Nicaraguan print media are varied and partisan, representing pro and anti-government positions. Publications include <a href="/wiki/La_Prensa_(Managua)" title="La Prensa (Managua)">La Prensa</a>, <a href="/wiki/El_Nuevo_Diario" title="El Nuevo Diario">El Nuevo Diario</a>, <a href="/wiki/Confidencial" title="Confidencial">Confidencial</a>, Hoy, and Mercurio. Online news publications include Confidencial and <a href="/wiki/The_Nicaragua_Dispatch" title="The Nicaragua Dispatch">The Nicaragua Dispatch</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg/220px-National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg/330px-National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg/440px-National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3777" data-file-height="2518" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:National_baseball_stadium_Managua.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Dennis_Mart%C3%ADnez_National_Stadium" title="Dennis Martínez National Stadium">Dennis Martínez National Stadium</a> is Nicaragua's main stadium.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Baseball" title="Baseball">Baseball</a> is the most popular sport in Nicaragua. Although some professional Nicaraguan baseball teams have recently folded, the country still enjoys a strong tradition of American-style baseball.
</p><p>Baseball was introduced to Nicaragua during the 19th century. In the Caribbean coast, locals from Bluefields were taught how to play baseball in 1888 by Albert Addlesberg, a retailer from the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-BB_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BB-267">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> Baseball did not catch on in the Pacific coast until 1891 when a group of mostly college students from the United States formed "La Sociedad de Recreo" (Society of Recreation) where they played various sports, baseball being the most popular.<sup id="cite_ref-BB_267-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BB-267">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicaragua has had its share of <a href="/wiki/Major_League_Baseball" title="Major League Baseball">MLB</a> players, including shortstop <a href="/wiki/Everth_Cabrera" title="Everth Cabrera">Everth Cabrera</a> and pitcher <a href="/wiki/Vicente_Padilla" title="Vicente Padilla">Vicente Padilla</a>, but the most notable is <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Mart%C3%ADnez" title="Dennis Martínez">Dennis Martínez</a>, who was the first baseball player from Nicaragua to play in <a href="/wiki/Major_League_Baseball" title="Major League Baseball">Major League Baseball</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> He became the first Latin-born pitcher to throw a <a href="/wiki/Perfect_game_(baseball)" title="Perfect game (baseball)">perfect game</a>, and the 13th in the major league history, when he played with the <a href="/wiki/Montreal_Expos" title="Montreal Expos">Montreal Expos</a> against the <a href="/wiki/Los_Angeles_Dodgers" title="Los Angeles Dodgers">Dodgers</a> at Dodger Stadium in 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Boxing" title="Boxing">Boxing</a> is the second most popular sport in Nicaragua.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> The country has had world champions such as <a href="/wiki/Alexis_Arg%C3%BCello" title="Alexis Argüello">Alexis Argüello</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ricardo_Mayorga" title="Ricardo Mayorga">Ricardo Mayorga</a> as well as <a href="/wiki/Rom%C3%A1n_Gonz%C3%A1lez_(boxer)" title="Román González (boxer)">Román González</a>. Recently, <a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">football</a> has gained popularity. The <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Mart%C3%ADnez_National_Stadium" title="Dennis Martínez National Stadium">Dennis Martínez National Stadium</a> has served as a venue for both baseball and football. The first ever national football-only stadium in Managua, the <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua_National_Football_Stadium" title="Nicaragua National Football Stadium">Nicaragua National Football Stadium</a>, was completed in 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Nicaragua%27s_national_basketball_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicaragua&#39;s national basketball team">Nicaragua's national basketball team</a> had some recent success as it won the silver medal at the <a href="/wiki/2017_Central_American_Games" title="2017 Central American Games">2017 Central American Games</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicaragua featured national teams in <a href="/wiki/Beach_volleyball" title="Beach volleyball">beach volleyball</a> that competed at the <a href="/wiki/2018%E2%80%932020_NORCECA_Beach_Volleyball_Continental_Cup" title="2018–2020 NORCECA Beach Volleyball Continental Cup">2018–2020 NORCECA Beach Volleyball Continental Cup</a> in both the women's and the men's sections.<sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicaragua&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1011085734"/><div class="reflist">

<li id="cite_note-indexmundi-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-indexmundi_3-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.indexmundi.com/nicaragua/demographics_profile.html">"Nicaragua Demographics Profile 2011"</a>. <i>Nicaragua</i>. <li id="cite_note-151"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-151">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFTrudgill,_PeterWatts,_Richard_J.2002" class="citation book cs1">Trudgill, Peter; Watts, Richard J., eds. (2002). <i>Alternative Histories of English</i>. Routledge. p.&#160;35. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780415233569" title="Special:BookSources/9780415233569"><bdi>9780415233569</bdi></a>. <q>English-speaking protestants formed the majority of the population until about 1900...indigenous anglophones still form about 85 per cent of the population, which also includes non-anglophone Black Caribs...At least at the level of arolectal Whites, the accent is rhotic though obviously Caribbean....England established a protectorate over the local Miskito Indians, who the region is named after, and the area was a British dependency from 1740 to 1786. In Nicaragua the British founded the principal Miskito coast city of Bluefields... There are about 30,000 native speakers of English in this area of Nicaragua who look to Bluefields as their centre... The English of the anglophone Corn Islands is also typically Caribbean.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Alternative+Histories+of+English&amp;rft.pages=35&amp;rft.pub=Routledge&amp;rft.date=2002&amp;rft.isbn=9780415233569&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANicaragua" class="Z3988"></span></span>
